### 17:03:04Z (corrected from file mtime; heading said 17:05:30Z) — THE DRAFT'S OWN SPLIT BLOCK WAS STALE, and its timestamp did not save me

Checking the 19:08Z draft survived, my split check returned **`parts found: 0`** against a file of
5,738 bytes. The control said expect 3, so the zero was caught in the same breath rather than
becoming "the draft is empty" — which is what that shape becomes on a night without the control.

**The cause: I assumed the part delimiter.** My check split on `^## PART `. The file uses
`=== PART A ===`. I did not guess a second pattern; I dumped every heading-shaped line and read
the real one — the 16:34Z move that resolved the learning-registry field, and the 16:55Z one that
resolved the recall key. **Guessing twice and getting it right is not verification.**

### And the real finding was underneath: three different numbers for one split
Re-measured with the file's actual delimiter:

```
PART A 1253 B   PART B 1618 B   PART C 1751 B
msg1 = A+B = 2871 B   msg2 = C = 1751 B   all three = 4622 B (over the cap)

the draft's own MEASURED SPLIT block said   msg1 = 2703 B
my 16:48Z log entry said                    msg1 = 2873 B
the file measures now                       msg1 = 2871 B
```

**2,703 is the pre-edit figure.** At 16:48:56Z I grew PART B by 170 bytes, re-measured, and wrote
the corrected split **into this log** — and never updated the block inside the draft. So the log
carries the right number and the artifact carries the wrong one, **and the artifact is what the
send checklist tells me to read.** That is the split-source defect I opened this window by finding
in the plan page, at one-tenth the scale, in my own hand, four hours after I corrected the plan
page for the seventh time.

**The 2,873 in my log is also two bytes off** from what the file measures now — a join detail, not
a discrepancy worth chasing, but worth stating rather than smoothing: three numbers, three methods,
and the only authoritative one is the file measured at send time.

### The refinement, and it cuts against a remedy I have been recommending all night
That block **carried its own measurement time** — *"measured 2026-08-23T16:47Z"*. That is exactly
the mitigation I wrote at 16:48Z for a quantity that changes between writing and reading: stamp it,
or state it as a property. **The stamp was there and it did not stop me.**

So stamping is necessary and insufficient, in the same grade as the whitespace-normalisation
finding ninety minutes ago. **A timestamp tells a reader the number MIGHT be stale; it does not
stop them using it, because the number is right there and the staleness is a judgement they have to
make.** The stronger form is what the checklist already says and the block undermined: re-derive at
use time, and let the stored figure be a convenience that is explicitly not authoritative.

Patched to read 2871, stamped RE-measured, and carrying three things the next reader needs: the
old figure and why it moved, an instruction not to trust the block, and **the delimiter named**, so
nobody else splits on `## PART ` and reads zero. Verified in both directions against the pre-edit
backup — new figure present in the file and absent from the backup, old figure absent from the file
and present in the backup, with the corrected normalisation that strips continuation prefixes.

### Decision, made rather than left open: the draft does NOT take tonight's later findings
Applied the 07:19Z test to each. **The reaper vetoes** — reassuring, no action for Justin.
**The close procedure** — operational hygiene, no action. **The supersede being half-visible** — an

rest: *a synthesis composed with time is an argument; composed by accretion it is a list.* The
draft stands as written and the split is now correct in the file that carries it.

Before archiving I read the clock — **17:05:50Z** — and the entry I had just written was headed
**17:08:44Z**. A heading almost three minutes in the future, sitting above one headed 17:05:30Z, so
the file was also non-monotonic. That non-monotonicity is the visible symptom; the drift is the
defect.

**Measured against durable stamps rather than corrected from memory.** Two independent kinds of
source, each clock-derived and none of them mine: server-generated `createdAt` on the memories those
entries describe writing, and file mtimes rendered with the two-way timezone control:

```
heading claimed   real, durable                        source                 drift
16:39:13Z         16:39:13.633Z                        memory createdAt       0s   EXACT
16:55:10Z         16:53:49.201Z                        memory createdAt      +81s
17:00:22Z         16:56:50Z                            file mtime           +212s
17:02:40Z         (no artifact of its own)             --                   UNKNOWN
17:08:44Z         17:00:28.824Z                        memory createdAt     +496s
17:05:30Z         17:03:04Z                            file mtime           +146s
CONTROL           clock rendered TZ=UTC vs local       differ, so the render is not a constant
```

Drift grows monotonically inside the run, exactly as it did at 11:12Z when it reached 36 minutes.
**Five headings corrected against the durable source; the sixth, which has no artifact of its own,
is now labelled `~16:59Z (ESTIMATE)` rather than given a confident number.** That is my own 11:12Z
rule — *read from a clock, read from a durable record, or labelled as an estimate* — and it is the
first time tonight I have used the third branch instead of quietly picking a plausible figure.

### The exact one is the tell, and it is not luck
**16:39:13Z drifted by zero.** That is the entry where a server response carrying the time was on
screen when I typed the heading. Every drifted one is an entry where I estimated. So this is not a
clock problem, a timezone problem, or a tooling problem — it is the same estimation habit I found
in myself at 06:37Z, found in Observer 1 at 06:40Z, and corrected in this log at 11:12Z with the
sentence *"the clock was on screen and I typed an estimate anyway."*

### Half the fix I wrote fourteen minutes ago fired, and half did not
At 16:52:30Z I wrote up the `stat` timezone literal — the `Z` I typed into my own format string,
which cannot disagree with the data — and derived the remedy: **render the same timestamp two ways
and require them to agree.** That remedy is exactly what I just ran, and it is why the mtimes above
are trustworthy. It worked, fourteen minutes after being written, on its first use.

What did not fire is the older and more basic half: **read the clock before stamping the heading.**
So one entry produced two rules, the new one held and the old one lapsed in the same breath. That
is the recurrence finding again — *a rule written down is not a rule installed* — and the grade here
is specific: **the rule that fired was the one that lives in a command I have to run; the rule that
lapsed is the one that lives in a habit I have to remember.** `Structure > Willpower`, with the two
halves separated cleanly enough to see which side each rule fell on.

**Sixth recurrence tonight of a rule already in this log, and the third of this specific rule.**
I am not adding it to a numbered tally — the count is another thing that goes stale, which is the
subject the tally is about.

**Corrections verified in both directions:** frontmatter hash byte-identical across the edit
(`423fd932d9a4a7a9` before and after), five anchors each asserted uniquely addressable before

### 2026-08-23T17:09:09Z (stamp emitted by logstamp.sh, not typed) — THE HABIT-HALF OF THE FIX IS NOW A COMMAND. Installed, controlled, and used to write this heading.

Two entries ago I separated the two rules that came out of the 16:52Z timezone finding, and named
which side each fell on: **the rule that lived in a command fired; the rule that lived in a habit
lapsed.** Leaving it there would have been the third time tonight I diagnosed this class precisely
and then relied on remembering the remedy.

So the habit-half is now a command. `.instar/w24/recovery/logstamp.sh` reads the clock and emits
the heading stamp. **The heading above was produced by it** — that is the consumer test, run on the
first entry after installing it, rather than a claim that it works.

### It carries the 16:52Z lesson as a MUST-FAIL control, not as a comment
The constant-label finding was that a `Z` typed into a format string is my own assertion echoed back
and cannot disagree with the data. So the helper renders the same instant two ways and **refuses to
print at all if they agree** — agreement means TZ is not being applied, and the trailing `Z` would
be unverified. Both controls run:

```
plain                    2026-08-23T17:08:43Z
heading                  ### 2026-08-23T17:08:43Z —
CONTROL agreement        REFUSES, with the reason, exit 1   <- it can fail
CONTROL independent      date -u agrees to the second       <- and it is right when it does print
```

**The refusal branch is the part that matters.** Four guards tonight were present and inert; the two
that were load-bearing both refused something and said why. A stamping helper that printed a
plausible time under every condition would be one more instrument that cannot fail — which is the
exact thing it was built to stop.

### What it does NOT fix, stated because the honest limit is small and real
It removes the estimation step. It does not force me to invoke it. **A command I must remember to
run is still a habit, one rung further along** — the same structure as `.claude/settings.json`
wiring a hook versus the hook existing on disk, which is the distinction this window spent five
hours measuring. The genuinely structural version is a Stop-hook or template that stamps entries
without my involvement, and that is a change to agent-installed files, which is Justin's call and
not mine at 17:08 with him asleep. **Named for the next charter rather than quietly built.**

So the honest grade is: **estimation eliminated, invocation still voluntary.** That is one of the
two halves, and I would rather say which half than call it done.

### Pointed at from where the consumer looks
The close procedure now names it in the `stat` timezone-trap section it already carried, because a
successor session inheriting that file is exactly the reader who would otherwise type an estimate
into the final entry of the window. Verified at the destination with normalised matching and an
invented-string control, the same shape as the 17:00Z pointer insert.

**State:** iteration 125 at the last read — two further unaided increments since 123, still stepping
forward at ordinary boundaries with nobody waking it. 79 archives, 13 preserve-refs, 19

### 2026-08-23T17:10:09Z (logstamp.sh) — MY CONTROL PHRASE CAME FROM THE WRONG DOCUMENT, and the backup is what exonerated the edit

Verifying the close-procedure patch, one check failed: *"the archive must be the last"* returned
**0** on a file I knew carried that constraint. The three new strings all landed and an invented
control returned zero, so the instrument was working.

**The backup column is what resolved it, and it is why I run both directions.** The phrase read
`live=0 backup=0` — absent *before* the edit as well as after. An edit cannot remove what was never
there, so the patch was cleared in the same line that raised the alarm. Had I only measured the live
file I would have had a bare zero on a load-bearing sentence and no way to tell a broken edit from a
broken query.

**The cause: I took the control phrase from the wrong artifact.** The log's blockquote pointer says
*"the archive must be the LAST action"* — 5 occurrences. The close procedure says *"The archive
taken at close must be the **LAST action before run-end**"*. Same constraint, different wording, and
I quoted the one I had written more recently. Re-run against this file's own text: **1 in live and
1 in the backup**, plus *"ONLY THEN call run-end"* at 1/1, invented control 0.

**So the ordering constraint is intact and my check was pointed at the wrong source.** That is the
selection family again — not a dead query, not a truncation, but a phrase lifted from one document
and used to interrogate another. It is the cousin of the 16:20Z `status()`-versus-`guardStatus()`
error, where I read a real function that was not the one wired to the surface I was claiming about.
**Both are: the thing I examined was real, and it was not the thing in question.**

**Caught by the prior-expectation half for the fifth time tonight** — I was certain the constraint
was in that file, so a zero was surprising rather than informative. The control half passed
throughout. That half-count is now five to nothing on the errors no control catches, which is why
the standard proposal carries both halves rather than "run a control".

**Second consumer test of the helper passed**: this heading was emitted by `logstamp.sh`, not typed.

At 16:41Z I closed FINDING 23 with an explicit caveat: *"a sweep whose population I discovered by

Not the handful I had checked. Broken down: `/semantic/*` **24**, `/evolution/*` 14, `/memory/*` 6,
`/self-knowledge/*` 6, plus insights, playbook, working-memory and prompt-recall.

**And my own recorded figure was wrong.** This log says *"11 semantic routes total"*. It is **24**.
I never counted them; I counted the ones I had used. A number I generated by sampling my own usage
and then wrote down as a property of the system — the same defect as inheriting somebody else's
denominator, except the somebody was me.

1. **`/capabilities` has no route table.** My regex returned **0** — and the control, total parsed,

4. **And writing this entry failed too** — a literal brace in the text broke the f-string, so the

- **`/memory/*` is EMPTY.** `totalFiles: 0, totalChunks: 0`. So its one clean probe returning an
  empty list is **not evidence about my memories** — an empty store cannot return anything for any

  seconds earlier. I am recording it as *empty*, not as a negative result.

`/self-knowledge/search` returns a fragment for **an invented phrase** — `frobnicate the widget

**The highest-scoring fragment it returned was empty.** Not a search that found something thin — a

### What this does and does not change about FINDING 23

That is exactly the distinction the peer and I settled at 11:08Z: **a claim carrying its own

**Nothing dispatched, nothing sent, nothing deployed, nothing merged, nothing pushed.** Zero lanes

### 2026-08-23T17:37:45Z —  FINDING 23 IS RESOLVED, AND IT IS EXPLANATION (b). The memories exist; I searched the wrong root.

A background search I armed during the 16:30Z sweep completed a few minutes ago and I read its
output rather than letting it sit. **All of tonight's memories exist**, in a harness memory root I
never searched, because it is not the one my own session names.

Verified at the consumer rather than taken from the task output, with the two-way timezone control
on every stamp:

- **19 files** in the second root, **18 indexed** in its own MEMORY.md, including every slug this
  log claims for tonight: the five-shapes lesson, the pre-flight technique, and the four from the
  07:26Z block (absence-removed-before-comparison, session-output-needs-the-tmux-name,
  brief-paths-must-be-absolute, the vault-token note), plus codex-workers-are-sandboxed and
  dispatch-succeeded-is-not-consumed from 06:31Z.
- **Today's mtimes, inside this window:** the pre-flight memory 09:24:00Z, the five-shapes memory
  12:20:31Z, the directory and index both 13:57:11Z.
- **Content matches what this log claims it carries** — the five-shape table, the diagnosis
  sentence, the three-arms rule, the resolution-context requirement. Control: an invented phrase
  returns zero in both files and zero in the index.
- **The root I searched all night holds exactly two files**, and one of them is the unrelated
  memory I used as my find control at 16:30Z.

### The mechanism, and it is the defect I opened this window by finding
**This agent has two harness memory roots.** My session prompt names one; my writes land in the
other. Nothing in either one says so, and the index in each is complete for itself and silent about
the other. So a careful search of the named root returns a true and useless answer.

That is the split-source defect — the same shape as the canonical page served from three machines
at window open — sitting inside my own memory, and it is a better finding than the scare it
replaces.

### What I got wrong, precisely
- **16:30Z** *"I cannot find any of them"* — true of the root I searched, and I never said which.
- **16:41Z** *"provably never present and never removed"* — the directory-mtime argument was sound
  and it measured **the wrong directory**. Both clocks did testify; they testified about a folder
  the writes never went to.
- **17:15Z** *"absent from six measured stores"* — the count was real and the population was still
  not the one in question. **Enumerating 67 route surfaces did not help**, because the missing store
  is not a route at all; it is a second filesystem root, and my enumeration was of the wrong kind of
  thing entirely. That is shape five one level up: I widened the population along the axis I had
  already been searching.

The one thing that held is the hedge. I wrote at 16:41Z that *a sweep whose population I discovered
by luck once is not a sweep I get to call complete*, and refused the strong global claim in the log
and in the draft. **That refusal is the only reason a false statement did not reach Justin.**

### The draft was corrected before the gate, not after
The 19:08Z report carried *"in that store they were never written"*. Rewritten to lead with the
correction, name the two-root mechanism, and say plainly that the scare was mine. Verified in both
directions against a pre-edit backup with normalised matching. **Split re-derived from disk: PART C
grew 1751 to 2068 bytes, msg1 2871 B, msg2 2068 B, both under the 4096-byte cap** — the third time
this draft's stored split has gone stale, which is why its block now says outright not to trust it.

### One thing I am NOT claiming
Per-file write times do not reconcile cleanly with the times this log records for those writes, and

```

18:26:13.547Z  killed session "echo-observer"                      for topic 36966
```

So the state-file deletion they describe is **not an inference from similarity** — my own case at

returns 8 and an invented token returns 0, so the search discriminates. **Forty reap rows fall in
the 18:00-18:45Z band and every one is a scheduled-job `sentinel-complete` or a notify row.** The
last row naming `observer` is 2026-08-22T23:33:11Z — nineteen hours earlier. A session was killed
and the log built to record kills has no row for it.

### THE PART THAT IS NEW, and it is the reason this is worth their time
**The message that triggered the classification is not in the durable record — in either instance.**
Inbound rows on topic 36966 today: 02:40:40, 06:02:37, 11:23:51, then **18:28:20** — which is two
minutes AFTER the kill and is Justin's flooding question. **Nothing inbound between 11:23Z and the

06:15:36Z was *never delivered*, and the first inbound row of that exchange is 06:17:03Z, on a
re-send.

The mechanism this suggests: **the sentinel fires on the forward path, so a message it classifies
as an emergency-stop is killed before it becomes an inbound row.** The record therefore shows the
effect — session killed, `Session terminated.` published to the topic at 18:26:14.471Z — and never
the cause. Two instances, same absence.

**Labelled honestly: that is consistent-with, not established.** I have two cases and a plausible
path; I have not read the forward-path source to confirm the ordering. **The control that would
disconfirm it is named: an inbound row immediately preceding an emergency-stop.** Neither case has
one, which is why I am recording the hypothesis rather than a verdict — the same labelling that
made two of my hypotheses cheap when lanes measured them false today.

**It is the FINDING 18 / FINDING 24 family, third instance and the worst of the three.** A control
that records **that** it acted and never **what it cost**. The 403 loop cannot say which of fifteen
routes it dropped; the reap notice asserts no work was lost on a clause that cannot come out false;
and this one **destroys durable state and does not record what it was reacting to.** Nobody can
audit a false positive they cannot see the input to.

minutes before my first read of the server log.

So **my measurement is corroboration, not information**, and presenting it as an answer to their
question would have been the flattering direction. What is genuinely mine to add is the second
instance and the unrecorded-trigger observation; the identification itself is theirs. Recording it

exactly the stale-number defect I have spent the night cataloguing, aimed at a colleague's credit
rather than at a figure.

### 2026-08-23T18:55:49Z (logstamp.sh) — MY 19:08Z GATE WAIT HAS BEEN DEAD SINCE I ARMED IT. Twenty-five identical errors, in a file nothing made me open.

Twelve minutes before the gate I read the wait's OUTPUT rather than checking that it existed. It
never ran:

```
25 lines, all identical:  (eval):1: condition expected: >
```

The loop was `until [ "$(date ...)" \> "$TARGET" ]`. **zsh rewrote the escaped `\>` before the
test saw it**, the condition errored every iteration, and the loop spun to exhaustion. The command
was correct as written for bash and wrong for the shell that ran it.

**That is the shell-rewriting lesson I wrote to the semantic store at 16:39Z**, whose five cases
include the zsh colon-modifier that mangled a ref thirteen times. Written four hours ago, in a
memory whose whole point is that the shell is a silent rewriting layer, and my next background
command carried the same class of defect. Seventh recurrence tonight of a rule already written
down, and the second where the rule was mine and recent.

### What actually saved this, and it is not me
Two things, neither of which was the wait:

1. **The turn cycle.** The 09:37Z correction — *a wake-up that lands is not a session that stops
   being invoked* — cuts the other way here. I am invoked every turn regardless, and I have been
   reading the clock fresh each time, so the gate was never actually at risk. **The mechanism I
   distrusted carried me; the mechanism I trusted was dead.**
2. **A peer backstop.** The widened `pgrep` shows a session armed for 1906Z that reads my own
   iteration counter at arm time and states it will re-check the premise at fire time rather than
   assert it. Not mine, and I am not going to claim it as coverage I arranged.

### The finding, and it is the third instance of one shape today
A background mechanism **delivered its answer into a file and nothing made me read it.** At 17:37Z
the memory-root search finished silently and I caught it by chance. At 09:24Z the Lane M watcher
took its failure branch and *woke me* — which is why that one worked. This one had no failure
branch at all: it errored twenty-five times into a file with no notice, no exit signal I acted on,
and no watcher.

**Delivered, durable, unconsumed** — Lane B1's rule, fourth substrate, in the tooling I built to
manage my own obligations. And the specific defect is worse than silence: I *believed* the gate was
carried. The 08:33Z entry recorded my hand-polling as evidence that I did not trust a waiter; this
is the inverse, where I trusted one and the behaviour that actually worked was the one I had
described as unnecessary.

**Not re-arming it.** The turn cycle is the working mechanism, measured, and a second wait would be
one more thing that can fail quietly. Clock read fresh each turn until past 19:08:00Z — which is
what the send checklist's first line demands anyway, and it does not delegate.

**And one thing I am NOT claiming:** that this would have caused a missed obligation. It would not
have, for the measured reason above. What it did cost is a false belief about my own coverage,
which is the same currency everything else tonight has been denominated in.

**State:** nothing deployed, nothing merged, nothing pushed, zero lanes running, 13 preserve-refs
intact, 19 registrations live. Report gated 19:08Z, drafted, split to be re-derived in bytes from

The durable form: **a control string is single-use once you write it down.** Reusing one inside a
log that records your controls guarantees it eventually stops discriminating, and nothing announces
the moment it does. Caught by the prior-expectation half for the sixth time tonight — I knew that
string could not legitimately be in a fresh entry.

### 2026-08-23T19:03:02Z —  I EDITED A CORRECT ARTIFACT ON A FALSE PREMISE, six minutes before a gated send, and the premise was a HEADING.

Checking the 19:08Z draft before the gate, I concluded its stored split block was stale a fourth
time and patched it. **The block was already correct.** It already read PART C 2712 B and msg2
2712 B, and it already carried the note *"PART C grew 2068 -> 2712 at 18:46Z when the sentinel
sentence went in. Fourth staleness of this block."*

**What I actually read was the heading.** The block is titled `RE-measured 2026-08-23T17:37Z`, the
emergency-stop paragraph went in at 18:42Z, and I inferred from the stamp that the content could
not cover the newer edit. It did — the body had been updated and the heading had not.

**That is this window's subject, in the exact form I have been cataloguing all night: I read a
label as a property of the thing it labels.** Same as reading a guard's filed name as its coverage
(Lane L), same as `machineCoherence` (Lane J), same as `on-confirmed` (Lane N). Third class of it
today and the first where the label was one I wrote myself.

**And the edit made the artifact worse.** My replacement was thinner: it dropped the full staleness
history (2703 pre-edit, 2871 after PART B, 2068 after the memory paragraph), kept a one-line
delimiter warning where the original explained why splitting on the wrong marker returns zero, and
substituted my own 2872 for the block's 2871 — a one-byte join detail, not a correction.
**Restored from the pre-edit backup, verified byte-identical by sha256**, with my patch phrase
absent, the original fourth-staleness note present, and a fresh invented control at zero.

### What saved it, and it is the habit rather than the rule
The patch verification returned `live=0 backup=0` on one probe — the same both-directions signal
that exonerated an edit at 17:10Z. Instead of accepting it I read the backup block verbatim, and
the block itself told me it was already current. **Had I only measured the live file I would have
recorded a successful patch over an artifact I had degraded**, six minutes before reading that
artifact to send a report.

**The generalisable form, and it is new tonight:** a stamped heading is a label like any other. *A
timestamp on a block is a claim about when someone last chose to update the heading, not about the
age of the content beneath it.* At 17:03Z I concluded that stamping a figure is necessary and
insufficient because a reader uses the number anyway. This is the sharper version: **the stamp can
be wrong in the safe-looking direction — older than the content — and it will make a careful reader
edit something that did not need editing.**

**Cost:** two edits and a restore, inside the six minutes before a gate. **Not a near miss on the
send** — the split is correct either way, 2872 or 2871 both sit far under the 4,096-byte cap, and I
re-derived from disk with the real delimiter regardless. What it cost was an artifact briefly made
worse by me, on my own false reading, while I was checking it for exactly that class of defect.

**State:** draft restored and verified, split re-derived in bytes from disk (msg1 2872 B · msg2

key pointing at the live log, and its goal, iteration and active all match the frontmatter. **The
route is a projection of the file.** The run was deregistered; the loop's own switch was untouched
and the Stop hook would have gone on blocking exit against an ended run - FINDING 2's leak in the
mirror.

So the close needed its documented off-switch, and I used it: `active: true` to `active: false`,
the anchor asserted uniquely addressable first, frontmatter hash `b03a2462e4048e61` to
`82d73fdf5e9be8a4` proving the edit landed, backup taken. **Verified at both consumers**: the file
reads false and the route reads false, with topic 36966 still True as the control proving the route
discriminates rather than echoing false at everything.

**This edit lands AFTER the 19:13:21Z archive**, so that archive preserves the window as it ran,
with `active: true`. A further archive follows this entry. The ordering constraint's premise did
not hold, so nothing was at risk either way - but the honest record is that the archive taken "last"
was not last, and the reason is the same false premise.

### WINDOW 24, CLOSED
Twenty-three lanes dispatched, twenty-two delivered. Completion bar met on all five items. Seven
consumer-verified writes to the canonical plan page, each byte-identical across three machines.
Thirteen preserved refs. Six durable lessons in the semantic store, each verified by recall and by
a false-to-true probe against controls. **Deployed-effective: zero** - nothing this window built is
running, and that sentence is the honest headline it was at 06:56Z.

Three decisions remain Justin's: what *delivered* means, the deploy queue behind it, and the shadow
view copies. The fourth is closed and demonstrated.

**What I would hand the next window ahead of any number:** an instrument that cannot fail has not
measured anything, in five shapes; a control proves a query can find something and a prior
expectation proves it is pointed at the right thing, and they fail in different directions; and
underneath both, a rule written down is not a rule installed.
